<!-- p. 6 -->
## 1 Introduction

### 1.1 Overview of results

The 2024 UK parliamentary general election was held on Thursday 4 July 2024. The election followed the dissolution of Parliament on 30 May. Voting was held in 650 constituencies across the UK: 543 in England, 57 in Scotland, 32 in Wales and 18 in Northern Ireland.

> General elections in the UK are held using a “first-past-the-post” voting system, with the candidate receiving the greatest number of votes in each of 650 constituencies winning that seat.

There were new constituency boundaries throughout the UK. 65 seats had no changes to their boundaries, although four of these had a new name. The remaining 585 seats had at least some changes.

The Labour Party, led by Sir Keir Starmer, won the largest number of seats and votes. Labour won the election with 411 seats and 34% of the vote across the UK. Labour’s vote share was 1.6 percentage points up on 2019. It received a lower vote share than any party forming a post–war majority government.

> The party with the most seats generally forms the government.

The incoming Labour government replaced the previous Conservative government. Boris Johnson led the Conservatives to victory at the previous general election in 2019. He was replaced as Prime Minister by Liz Truss in September 2022. After Truss’s resignation in October 2022, the Conservative government was led by Prime Minister Rishi Sunak until the 2024 general election.

The Conservatives won 121 seats in 2024. This was the lowest number of seats the party had won at any general election since 1832. The Conservatives’ 24% vote share was 20 percentage points down on 2019 and lower than at any general election since 1832. They won 244 fewer seats than at the 2019 election.

The Liberal Democrats won 72 seats with 12.2% of the vote in 2024. This was 61 seats more than in 2019, with an increased vote share of 0.7 percentage points. The number of seats was higher than at any general election since 1923, when the Liberals won 158 seats. While the Liberal Democrat vote share was higher in 2024 than in 2015, 2017 and 2019, it was lower than their post-war high in 2010, when they won 23% of the vote. The Liberal Democrats <!-- p. 7 --> became the third largest party in the House of Commons for the first time since they ceded this position to the SNP in 2015.

Reform UK won five seats. Reform UK was the new name of the Brexit Party. Reform UK had 609 candidates in 2024 and received 14.3% of the vote across Great Britain. In 2019, the Brexit Party had 275 candidates because it did not stand in the 317 seats previously won by the Conservatives. In 2019, the Brexit Party had averages of 5.1% in the seats where they had candidates.

> Every candidate in a general election is required to submit a £500 deposit, which is returned if they receive more than 5% of total votes in their constituency. In 2024, 1,660 candidates (37% of the 4,515 total) lost their deposit. This included 30 Conservative, 1 Labour, 227 Liberal Democrat, 350 Green and 31 Reform UK candidates who lost.

The Scottish National Party (SNP) won nine of the 57 seats in Scotland, compared with 48 of 59 in 2019.

Plaid Cymru won four of the 32 seats in Wales and 14.8% of the Welsh vote, compared with four of 40 seats and 9.9% of the vote in 2019. Plaid Cymru’s 2024 vote share in Wales was its highest since it started contesting general elections in 1929.

The Green Party of England and Wales won four seats, the first time they have returned more than one MP to Westminster. Across the UK, Green parties received 6.7% of the vote, compared with 2.7% in 2019.

In Northern Ireland, Sinn Féin won seven seats, the same as in 2019. The Democratic Unionist Party (DUP) won five, losing three of the seats they won in 2019. The SDLP won two seats and the Alliance Party, Traditional Unionist Voice and the Ulster Unionist Party won one seat each. Neither of the latter two parties had MPs in the previous Parliament.

### 1.2 Turnout

Turnout was 59.7%, down from 67.3% in 2019. The total registered electorate was 48.2 million, 600,000 more than the 47.6 million registered electorate in 2019. Turnout was lower than general elections between 2005 and 2019, but just higher than the 59.4% turnout in the 2001 general election.

### 1.3 MP leavers and joiners

132 MPs did not stand for re-election at the 2024 general election. This included 75 Conservative MPs, 34 Labour MPs and 9 Independent MPs, based on party affiliation at the end of the 2019–24 Parliament.

In 2024, 350 MPs were elected who had not been in the 2019–24 Parliament, although 15 of them had been MPs at some point in the past.This meant that 335 of those elected were new to Parliament. Previously, the highest number

```text
[chart/graphic text beside the paragraphs above]
1
```

[^1]: House of Commons Library, MPs not standing in the 2024 General Election , 10 June 2024

<!-- p. 8 -->
of new MPs at a general election since 1918 was in 1945, when there were 327 new MPs.

218 MPs from the previous Parliament stood unsuccessfully in the 2024 general election, including 175 former Conservative MPs, 28 former SNP MPs and four former Labour MPs. 300 MPs from the previous Parliament were re-elected, including 95 Conservative MPs, 168 Labour MPs and 15 Liberal Democrats MPs.

263 women were elected in 2024.This was 40% of the total, and the highest number ever. Following the 2024 election, 46% of Labour MPs are women, compared with 24% of Conservative MPs and 44% of Liberal Democrat MPs.

According to the British Future think tank , 90 MPs with a minority ethnic background were elected in 2024: 25 more than in 2019. By party, those MPs were: 66 Labour, 15 Conservative and 5 Liberal Democrat. This means that 14% of MPs are from a minority ethnic background; and 16% of Labour MPs; 12% of Conservative MPs; and 7% of Liberal Democrat MPs.

The most senior MP (based on continuous service and order sworn in) is the Conservative MP Sir Edward Leigh who has been an MP continuously since 1983. He becomes the “Father of the House”. Labour MP, Diane Abbott, was first elected in 1987, and has been given the honorific “Mother of the House” title.

The SNP’s 9 seats and 30.0% vote share in 2024 were both lower than any general election since 2015.

The SNP lost 36 seats to Labour and four to the Liberal Democrats; the SNP gained one seat from the Conservatives, Aberdeenshire North and Moray East.

In the House of Commons, the SNP had been the third largest party since 2015; in 2024 it ceded this position to the Liberal Democrats.

### 2.8 Plaid Cymru

```text

[rotated axis labels: 2010 2015 2017 2019 2024 2005 2010 2015 2017 2019]
                                               notional results
```

Plaid Cymru’s 14.8% vote share is their highest ever at a general election.

Plaid Cymru won 4 seats, the same number as in the 2019 election. However, after the constituency boundary changes which reduced the number of seats in Wales from 40 to 32, the notional results estimated that Plaid Cymru would have won only 2 seats had the 2019 election been held on the new boundaries. This means that the party is recorded as having gained two seats relative to the 2019 notional results.

### 2.9 Sinn Féin / Democratic Unionist Party

#### Sinn Féin

Sinn Féin won 7 seats, the same as in 2019 and 2017. Sinn Féin’s 27.0% vote share was up on 2019, but lower than in 2017, although the party did have 4 fewer candidates than in 2017. Sinn Féin became the largest party in Northern Ireland in 2024.

<!-- p. 24 -->
## 3 Election results by country and region

### 3.1 North East England

**Seats won in 2024 (net change since 2019 notional results):** LAB 26 (+7) · CON 1 (-7)

Seven seats changed hands in the North East, all Labour from Conservative.

The Conservative party's single seat in the North East is Stockton West. Labour represents the other 26 seats in the region.

Reform UK’s 19.9% was its highest regional vote share.

The Liberal Democrat’s 5.9% and the Green Party’s 6.0% were their lowest shares in any English region.

*[Original page also shows maps of the region's winners in 2015, 2017, 2019 and 2024, a line chart of vote share by party 2001–2019 and a stacked bar chart of seats won by party 2001–2024 — see PDF p. 24.]*

### 3.6 East of England

**Seats won in 2024 (net change since 2019 notional results):** LAB 27 (+22) · CON 23 (-32) · LD 7 (+6) · Reform 3 (+3) · Green 1 (+1)

The Conservatives lost 32 seats in the East of England, 22 to Labour, six to the Liberal Democrats, two to Reform UK and one to the Green Party.

Labour gained 22 seats in the region. The 27 seats held by Labour is their highest ever in the region.

26% of MPs in the East of England are women, compared to 40% for the whole UK. East of England has the lowest ratio of any UK country or region.

### 3.7 London

**Seats won in 2024 (net change since 2019 notional results):** LAB 59 (+7) · CON 9 (-11) · LD 6 (+3) · Others 1 (+1)

12 of London’s 75 seats changed hands. The Conservatives lost 11 seats, 8 to Labour and three to the Liberal Democrats; Labour lost one seat to an independent.

Labour’s vote share in London fell by 5.2% points on 2019; although its 59 London MPs is the highest since 1966 when 64 of the 102 Greater London MPs were Labour.

None of London’s remaining nine Conservative seats are in Inner London. Over half (51%) London MPs are women, the only UK country or region where this is the case.

*[Original page also shows maps of the region's winners in 2015, 2017, 2019 and 2024, a line chart of vote share by party 2001–2019 and a stacked bar chart of seats won by party 2001–2024 — see PDF p. 30.]*

### 3.11 Wales

**Seats won in 2024 (net change since 2019 notional results):** LAB 27 (+9) · LD 1 (+1) · Plaid Cymru 4 (+2) · CON 0 (-12)

The boundary review resulted in Wales having eight fewer seats than at the previous election.

Based on notional results which allow for the boundary changes, 12 seats in Wales changed hands in 2024; Labour gained nine, Plaid Cymru two and the Liberal Democrats one, all from the Conservatives.

The Conservatives have no MPs in Wales. This happened previously in 1997, for the first time, and 2001.

### 3.12 Northern Ireland

**Seats won in 2024 (net change since 2019 notional results):** DUP 5 (-3) · Sinn Féin 7 · SDLP 2 · Alliance 1 · UUP 1 (+1) · Others 2 (+2)

## 4 Analysis of the election and its results

### 4.1 Candidates

A total of 4,515 candidates stood for election on 4 July 2024, the highest number since 1918. The chart below shows the change in the total number of candidates at UK general elections since 1918.

#### Candidates by party

Five parties fielded candidates in over 600 constituencies, as shown in the chart below.

The Conservative Party had 635 candidates, the highest number of any party in this election and the same as in 2019. This total included candidates for 630 constituencies in Great Britain. In Northern Ireland, the Conservative Party had candidates in five out of the 18 constituencies.

The Labour Party had 631 candidates, one in every constituency within Great Britain (excluding Chorley, where the Speaker was seeking re-election) – the same number as in 2019.

The Liberal Democrats fielded 630 candidates in Great Britain only, 19 more than in 2019. Green Parties put forward 629 candidates across the UK, 131 more than in 2019. Reform fielded candidates in 609 seats in Great Britain.

The SNP fielded candidates in each of the 57 constituencies in Scotland and Plaid Cymru candidates stood in all 32 constituencies in Wales.

518 candidates represented ‘other’ parties (including the Speaker) across the UK and 459 stood as independents.

Richmond and Northallerton constituency had the highest number of candidates (13), followed by the three constituencies of Ealing Southall, Holborn and St Pancras and Oxford East, which each had 12 candidates. Richmond and Northallerton is Rishi Sunak’s constituency and Holborn and St Pancras is Sir Keir Starmer’s constituency.

In Chorley (where the Speaker was seeking re-election), there were five candidates: the Speaker, the Green Party, Democracy for Chorley, the English Constitution Party and the Trade Unionist and Socialist Coalition. #### Gender of candidates

There were around 1,385 female candidates , representing 31% of all candidates.

In 2024, the Labour Party had the highest proportion of female candidates (47%), followed by the Green Party (44%) and the SNP (39%). Reform had the lowest proportion of female candidates (16%) among parties fielding more than 30 candidates.

### 4.2 MPs standing down and former MPs standing again

132 MPs chose not to stand again after the dissolution of Parliament for the 2024 general election. This number included 75 Conservative MPs, 34 Labour MPs, 9 SNP MPs and 9 independents.

The total of 132 was more than the 76 MPs who stood down at the 2019 election and was also higher than most earlier elections. An average of 89 MPs stood down from the House of Commons at general elections between 1979 and 2019.

550 former MPs sought re-election in July 2024. 518 of these former MPs were sitting at the time of dissolution, of whom 300 were returned. 218 sitting MPs were defeated in 2024.

The chart below shows the number of MPs leaving the House of Commons at every general election since 1979.

### 4.3 New constituency boundaries and the boundary review

Following the 2023 boundary review , the 2024 election was held on a new set of constituency boundaries, for the first time since 2010. You can read more about this process in our research briefing Constituency boundary reviews and the number of MPs .

Constituency boundaries are reviewed periodically to make sure that constituencies are all a similar size and respect local ties between areas. The reviews alter constituencies to reflect rising and falling electorates and changes in the boundaries of the council electoral wards (smaller areas represented by elected local councillors) that comprise them.

As before, the boundary review resulted in the UK being divided into 650 constituencies. However, the distribution of seats across the UK changed. England’s allocation of seats rose by 10 to 543; Scotland’s fell by two to 57; Wales’s fell by eight to 32; and Northern Ireland’s allocation remained unchanged at 18.

<!-- p. 42 -->
All but 65 seats (10%) had some changes to their boundaries at the 2024 election. However, over half of constituencies remained very similar to their predecessors (with over 90% overlap in premises).

In some cases, the name of a constituency changed even though its boundaries did not (for example, Burton became Burton and Uttoxeter, but retained the same boundaries).

Conversely, some constituencies retained the same name even though their boundaries changed substantially. For example, Newcastle upon Tyne North contains only around 40% of the population of the constituency with that name before the boundary change, with the remainder coming from parts of the previous Newcastle East, Newcastle Central, and North Tyneside seats.

You can view changes in boundaries for individual constituencies on our interactive map .

Changes to constituency boundaries mean that comparison of constituency election results between 2019 and 2024 is not direct. As election results are only collected at constituency level, and not for smaller areas like wards, we do not know exactly what the 2019 election would have looked like based on the new areas. However, experts produced “notional results” for the 2019 election which provide an estimate of what the results of the 2019 election would have been if it had been held on the 2024 boundaries. These estimates give the Conservatives 372 seats for the 2019 election (+7 on their actual 2019 total), and Labour 200 (-2).

These notional results are commissioned by the BBC, ITV, Sky News and the Press Association, and produced by Colin Rallings, Michael Thrasher, David Denver, and Nicholas Whyte. You can view the notional results in full on our website .

### 4.4 Characteristics of those elected

The 2024 general election returned the highest number and proportion of female MPs ever recorded: 263 (40%) of 650 MPs elected were women.

It is estimated that 90 MPs elected in 2024 are from minority ethnic groups, up from 66 MPs in 2019, meaning that 14% of MPs are from a minority ethnic background.

#### Gender

The number of women elected in 2024 was 263 (40% of the total), the highest ever. Following the 2024 election, 46% of Labour MPs are women. This compares with 24% of Conservative MPs and 44% of Liberal Democrat MPs. The chart below shows the total number of female MPs returned at general elections by party since 1918.

129 (39%) of the 335 MPs elected for the first time in 2024 were women. Of these, 31% of new Conservative MPs were women, 40% of new Labour MPs were women and 38% of new Liberal Democrat MPs were women. Eight of 15 (53%) of re-elected former MPs were women.

Of the current 263 female MPs, 190 are Labour (46% of all Labour MPs, down from 51% in 2019); 29 are Conservative (24% of all Conservative MPs, the same as in 2019) and 32 are Liberal Democrat (44% of all Liberal Democrat MPs, down from 64% in 2019). There are 12 women MPs among other party groups.

Including those elected in 2024, a total of 693 women have been elected to the House of Commons since 1918.

Of 650 MPs elected at the 2024 general election, 52% (335) had no previous House of Commons experience. 56% of Labour MPs were new to Parliament in 2024, as were 76% of Liberal Democrat MPs.

Around 10% of current MPs were first elected before the 2010 general election, and a further 10% at the 2010 general election or by-elections before 2015. 80% of MPs were first elected since 2017.

There were 23 MPs who were elected in by-elections during the 2019–24 Parliament. All of them stood in the 2024 general election; five lost and 18 held their seat. Constituency boundary changes may have meant the seats at the general election were different from the by-elections. All Liberal Democrat and Labour by-election winners won again in the 2024 general election. The remaining five of the 23 winners stood again in 2024 but lost their seats: three Conservative candidates, one SNP candidate and one Workers Party of Britain candidate.

**Father of the House**

The most senior MP (based on continuous service and order sworn in) is the Conservative MP Sir Edward Leigh, who has been an MP continuously since 1983. He becomes the “Father of the House” as he first swore in as an MP before other MPs with continuous service since 1983.

**Mother of the House**

Labour MP, Diane Abbott, was first elected in 1987 and has been given the honorific “Mother of the House” title.

**‘Baby of the House’**

At 22 years old, the youngest MP to be elected at the general election was Sam Carling (Lab) in North West Cambridgeshire.

<!-- p. 46 -->
#### Other characteristics

**Minority ethnic groups**

According to the British Future think tank, 90 MPs with minority ethnic backgrounds were elected in 2024, 25 more than in 2019. By party, those MPs in 2024 were: 66 Labour, 15 Conservative and 5 Liberal Democrat. This means that 14% of MPs are from a minority ethnic background (16% of Labour MPs, 12% of Conservative MPs and 7% of Liberal Democrat MPs).

10% of male MPs and 19% of female MPs are from a minority ethnic background.

The Labour Party had the highest number of minority ethnic MPs with 66 MPs (16% of all Labour MPs). The Conservatives had 15 minority ethnic MPs (12%), while the Liberal Democrats had five (7%). Of the six independent MPs, 4 were from a minority ethnic background.

Nearly all the minority ethnic MPs elected in 2024 stood in constituencies in England. Just one minority ethnic MP was elected in each of Scotland and Wales. In Wales, Kanishka Narayan’s election in Vale of Glamorgan made him Wales’s first ever minority ethnic MP.

**Secondary and university education**

The Sutton Trust analysed the educational backgrounds of MPs elected in July 2024 .

Based on available information, the proportion of privately educated MPs is at a record low. In 2019, 29% of MPs were privately educated, compared with 7% of the UK population. In 2024, 23% of MPs attended private schools. The number of privately educated MPs was lowest among the Labour Party (15%) and highest among Conservatives (46%). 30% of MPs from the Liberal Democrats were privately educated.

Of all MPs, 63% went to comprehensive schools and 13% went to selective state grammar schools. Since 1979, the proportion of privately educated MPs has decreased by 18 percentage points. The largest decrease has occurred among Conservative Party MPs (27 points), closely followed by the Liberal Democrats (25 points), as shown in the chart below.

Nine out of 10 (90%) of MPs are university graduates and 40% hold a postgraduate qualification. 20% of MPs hold a degree from Oxford or Cambridge, down from 23% in 2017 and 21% in 2019. By comparison, 1% of the UK population is estimated to have attended Oxford or Cambridge. A further 34% of MPs went to another Russell Group university, compared with 6% of the UK population. There are more Oxford alumni (77) among 2024 MPs than graduates of Cambridge (47). Of all MPs elected in 2024, 42 attended a private school, followed by Oxford or Cambridge for their undergraduate studies. <!-- p. 48 -->
**Religion**

Comprehensive information on the religious beliefs of MPs is not available.

It has been reported that 25 Muslim MPs were elected in 2024, around 4% of all MPs. They are from the following parties: two Conservative, 19 Labour and four independent. There are estimated to be 14 Jewish, 12 Sikh, and 9 Hindu MPs.[^9]

Humanists UK have noted that around 40% of MPs chose to take a secular affirmation rather than a religious oath at swearing-in after the 2024 election.[^10]

**Disability**

There is no official monitoring of disability among candidates or MPs, but according to research by the Disability Policy Centre, the number of disabled MPs elected in 2024 is thought to be 12. These are: seven Labour, two Conservatives, two Liberal Democrats and one Plaid Cymru MP.

**LGBT+**

According to Pink News, 64 openly LGBT+ MPs were elected in 2024. This was three less than the high of 67 prior to the election. Of the 64, 50 were Labour, five Conservative, seven Liberal Democrat, one SNP and one Green. 18 of the LGBT+ MPs are women.

Of the 64 LGBT+ MPs elected in 2024, 30 were incumbents and 34 were new MPs.[^12]

Source: Ipsos, How Britain voted in the 2024 election

These are estimates based on people’s answers to a post-election survey immediately after polling day, and are subject to a margin of uncertainty. They do, however, provide insight into how different groups voted which is not available from the (secret) ballot itself.

In 2024, age continued to be a important dividing line. Labour led among every age group except those aged 65+, where the Conservatives had a 20 percentage point lead over Labour. Labour’s highest share was among 25-to-34-year-olds (47%). Labour did less well among 18-to-24-year-olds (41%), where the Greens took their highest age group share (20%). The Conservatives’ highest share was 43% of those aged 65 or over.

Labour’s votes were split relatively evenly between men and women. Women were more likely to vote Conservative than men, while a higher proportion of men (17%) voted Reform UK than women (13%).

In 2024, social class was not a reliable indicator of whether someone is more likely to vote for Labour and the Conservatives, unlike previous elections. Both parties had a similar mix of voters from different social classes. Reform UK did best among social class C2 (25%), equalling Conservative support among this group.[^14]

Labour led the Conservatives for most groups with the exception of:

those aged 65 or over (20% Conservative lead over Labour)

people who owned (outright) their houses (+10%)

people with no qualifications (+11%)

people who voted ‘leave’ in the 2016 EU Referendum (+19%).

Labour’s lead over the Conservatives was strongest for:

younger age groups (+36% for those aged 18 to 24, +37% for those aged 25 to 34);

voters living in rented property (+31%)

minority ethnic (+29%) and, particularly, Black (+52%) voters

voters with a degree (+24%)

Remain voters (+28%).

<!-- p. 51 -->
#### How did voters change party in 2024?

According to YouGov’s analysis, Labour held on to 71% of their 2019 voters, losing 10% to the Greens and 8% to the Lib Dems. Only 2% of 2019 Labour voters switched to the Conservatives.

The Conservatives held on to around half (53%) their 2019 voters in 2024. A quarter (25%) switched to Reform UK, while one in ten (10%) voted Labour and 7% for the Lib Dems.

**Which party did you vote for in the 2024 general election?**

### 4.6 Constituency demographics and voting behaviour

This section looks at voting patterns in constituencies with different demographic profiles in terms of age, ethnicity and religion.

Section 4.5 of this briefing looks at what polling tells us about UK-wide voting patterns in particular demographic groups. Looking at constituency-level trends is another way of examining how these trends played out in practice.

Constituency results are affected by a range of demographic and non-demographic factors. Not everyone in a constituency can or does vote, so the demographics of the constituency aren’t always the same as the demographics of the voters. Voting patterns amongst relatively small groups

```text
[chart/graphic text beside the paragraphs above]
15
```

[^15]: YouGov How Britain Voted in the 2024 general election 8 July 2024

<!-- p. 52 -->
of people can be ‘drowned out’ if a different, larger group tends to vote differently.

All of this means that constituency-level results relating to a demographic group don’t necessarily correspond with how people in that group voted at an individual level.

#### Age

Constituencies with larger populations of 18-to-24-year-olds tended to have a higher proportion of votes for Labour. 16 of the top 20 constituencies with the largest populations of 18-to-24-year-olds were won by Labour.

Four of the ‘youngest’ constituencies by this measure were won by other parties. The Green Party gained the Bristol Central seat from Labour and held Brighton Pavilion. Bath was held by the Liberal Democrats and Labour lost the Leicester South seat to an independent candidate (Shockat Adam).

Although Labour won in the majority of seats with large populations of 18to-24-year-olds (as shown by the chart below), these seats also experienced a fall in Labour’s vote share compared with the notional results for 2019. The second chart shows how seats where fewer than one in 10 people were aged 18 to 24 experienced an increase in Labour’s vote share. In seats where more than one in 10 people were in this age group, Labour’s vote share fell (albeit generally from a high level, and with Labour still retaining the seat).

On average, constituencies where fewer than one in 10 people were Muslim had a higher percentage of votes going to Labour in 2024 than in 2019. But in constituencies where more than one in 10 people were Muslim, the percentage of votes going to Labour fell. The larger the Muslim population, the more pronounced this effect was.

As with ‘young’ constituencies, Labour’s vote share fell substantially in areas where it previously had a large vote share, and in most cases Labour still won the seat.

### 4.7 The electorate

On polling day, the UK electorate was 48.25 million, 1.5% higher than the 2019 figure (47.56 million). Across the UK the average constituency electorate was 74,236.

Among the constituent countries of the UK, Northern Ireland had the largest average electorate (75,776) and Wales the smallest (73,392). Manchester Central was the constituency with the largest electorate (85,204), and Na h-Eileanan An Iar had the smallest (21,325).

Average electorate size per constituency differed across seats won by each party. Labour seats were slightly smaller on average (74,099) and Conservative seats were slightly larger on average (74,751). Constituencies won by the Liberal Democrats were similar to the UK average in terms of electorate size (74,239).

The four constituencies won by Plaid Cymru had the smallest average electorate of any party (68,969), partly because they won one of the five protected island seats in the UK which have lower electorates (Ynys Môn).

### 4.8 Turnout

> Turnout is measured here as the number of valid votes cast, measured as a percentage of the registered electorate.

Turnout at the 2024 general election was 59.7%, which was the lowest at a general election since 2001. Turnout was 7.6 percentage points lower than in 2019.This represented the largest drop in turnout between elections since between 1997 and 2001.

The chart below shows turnout at general elections since 1918, during which time it has varied between 57.2% (1918) and 83.5% (1950). Since the drop in 2001, turnout has never fully returned to the levels between the 1922 and 1997.

**Turnout by constituency and party**

The highest constituency-level turnout was in Harpenden and Berkhamsted (75.2%) in the East of England, which was a Liberal Democrat gain from the Conservative Party. Nine out of the 10 seats with the highest turnout changed hands, and seven of these were Liberal Democrat gains from the Conservatives, largely in the South East and South West. Twelve of the 20 seats with the highest turnout were Liberal Democrat gains.

<!-- p. 61 -->
The lowest turnout was 40.0% in Manchester Rusholme, which was the lowest constituency turnout recorded at a general election since South Staffordshire in 2005 (37.2%). Seventeen of the 20 constituencies with the lowest turnout were seats held by Labour and three quarters of these seats were in the North West, Yorkshire and the Humber or the West Midlands.

The largest decline in turnout compared with 2019 was a fall of 18.8 percentage points in East Ham. The top six biggest declines in turnout were all in London seats.

### 4.9 Invalid votes

Ballot papers are rejected if they are unmarked or marked incorrectly (for example if the voter voted for too many candidates, or if it is unclear who the voter intended to vote for).

116,063 votes cast at the 2024 general election were rejected as invalid at the count, a 1.6% decrease on the 117,919 votes rejected at the 2019 count.

Chorley, the constituency of the House of Commons Speaker (Sir Lindsay Hoyle) had the largest number of invalid votes at this election: 1,198 (one for every 28 valid votes cast in the constituency). In line with convention, the Conservatives, Labour and the Liberal Democrats did not field candidates in the Speaker’s constituency.

The tally of 1,198 invalid votes in Chorley was almost seven times higher the national average (179 invalid votes per constituency). This is the fourth general election in a row at which the largest number of invalid votes was in the constituency of a Speaker seeking re-election. In 2015 and 2017 this was Buckingham (John Bercow) and in 2019 it was also Chorley.

Six of the 20 constituencies with the highest proportion of invalid votes were in Yorkshire and the Humber.

North Cornwall had the lowest proportion of invalid vote papers of any constituency with one invalid ballot for every 1,090 valid votes cast.

Although the number of invalid votes was lower than 2019, it is still historically high. Since 1964, when data on invalid votes was first collated, the number has only been higher in 1979 and 2019.

### 4.10 Marginal and safe seats

The 2024 general election produced a large rise in the number of very marginal seats. 115 seats were won by a margin of 5% or less, 48 more than the 67 seats won by this margin in the 2019 election.

Correspondingly, the proportion of very safe seats fell: the number of seats won with a majority of over 50% of votes cast was five in 2024, down from 37

#### The most marginal seats

The table below lists the 40 most marginal seats in percentage terms. In all of these seats, the winning margin was less than 2% (there were 46 seats in total which had a margin within this range).

Most of these seats had been won by a relatively high margin in 2019. In 2019, all but seven of them had been won by a margin of over 20% of the vote, and

```text
over half (25 seats) had been won by more than 30% . The biggest
```

<!-- p. 66 -->
Conservative majority of 50.3% (24,180 votes) in 2019 was over-turned and won by Labour with a majority of 1.4% (630 votes).

Thirty-three of the 40 most marginal seats in 2024 (see the table below) were seats which were won by the Conservative Party in 2019. Of these, the Conservatives held 11 and lost 22. The tightest contest was in Hendon, London, which was a Labour gain from the Conservatives with a margin of just 0.04%, or 15 votes. Poole in the South West was also won by a small margin of 0.04% (18 votes). North West Cambridgeshire in the East of England was won by a similar margin of 0.09% (39 votes).

The seat held by the smallest margin was Basildon and Billericay in the East of England, which was won by the Conservative candidate by 20 votes (0.05% of the total). This seat had one of the biggest falls in winning margin compared with 2019, when it had been won by the former Conservative candidate by 20,749 votes (a 44.3% margin).

#### The safest seats

The seat with the biggest margin was Chorley, the seat of the Speaker of the House of Commons, which conventionally is not contested by the largest parties when the Speaker runs for re-election. The table below lists the 40 safest seats plus the Speaker’s seat, ranked by the winning margin as a percentage of the total vote.

After that, the 40 safest seats were won by between 36.8% and 56.5% of the vote. In all but one of these, the result was that the incumbent party held the seat. 16 of the safest seats were in London and 15 in the North West.

<!-- p. 67 -->
In 34 of the 40 safest seats the result was a Labour hold. Most of the safest seats in 2024 had not ranked among the safest in 2019. ### 4.11 Second places

In 219 (53%) of the seats won by Labour, the Conservatives were in second place. 87 of the (83%) of the Conservative-won seats had Labour in second place. In total, 47% of the UK’s 650 seats had Labour and Conservatives candidates in first and second place. In 2019, it was 71% and in 2017 89%.

<!-- p. 68 -->

While 53% of Labour’s 411 winners had Conservatives in second, in around one in five (22%) Reform UK was in second place and the Greens or SNP each were second in 9%.

Of the 72 seats won by the Liberal Democrats, 64 (89%) had Conservatives in second place and 6 (8%) were SNP in second.

#### How many times were Reform and the Greens in second place?

Reform came in second place in 98 constituencies. In 89 of these cases, it was second to Labour. 60 of these were in the north of England and 13 were in Wales.

By comparison, the Brexit Party came in second place in three constituencies in 2019, while UKIP came second in 120 constituencies in 2015.

The Green Parties came in second place in 40 constituencies, 18 of which were in London. In all but one of these seats it was second to Labour. The Greens came second in three seats in 2019, none in 2017 and five in 2015.

The map on the following page shows which party came in second place in each constituency.

### 4.12 Performance of Reform and the Greens

Reform UK – the new name of the Brexit Party – won five seats, had 609 candidates and received 14.3% of the vote in the 2024 general election.

The Green Party of England and Wales, Scottish Green Party and Green Party Northern Ireland are separate entities. Considered together, they had 629 candidates and 6.7% of the vote share, with the Green Party of England and Wales winning four seats.

For both Reform and the Greens, this was the highest number of seats they had ever won.

#### Vote share and seat share

Both Reform and the Greens had a considerably larger share of votes than seats won in Parliament. Reform ranked third in vote share with half a million more votes than the Liberal Democrats, who had the third highest seat share. This is shown in the chart below.

**Share of vote and share of seats in Parliament**

```text
                       Vote share                             Seat share
Labour                 33.7%                                  63.2%
Conservative           23.7%                                  18.6%
Reform UK              14.3%                                  0.8%
Liberal Democrats      12.2%                                  11.1%
Green parties          6.7%                                   0.6%
SNP                    2.5%                                   1.4%
DUP                    0.6%                                   0.8%
Plaid Cymru            0.7%                                   0.6%
Sinn Féin              0.7%                                   1.1%
Others                 4.9%                                   1.8%
```

This gap between vote share and seat share in 2024 was the most disproportionate on record . General elections in the UK use a “first past the post” voting system, with the candidate receiving the greatest number of votes in each of 650 constituencies winning that seat. This means there is no guarantee that the number of seats that a party wins will reflect the percentage share of votes cast for that party.

#### How did Reform and Green performance compare with previous elections?

The Reform vote share (14.3%) in 2024 is similar to the UK Independence Party (UKIP) vote share (12.6%) in the 2015 general election when it was led by Nigel Farage, when it won one seat.

<!-- p. 71 -->
In 2019, the Brexit Party won 2% of the vote share and won no seats. However, it only put forward 275 candidates as it did not stand in the 317 seats previously won by the Conservatives . The party won an average of 5.1% of the vote in seats where they stood candidates.

The Green Party won a seat for the first time at the 2010 general election with 1.0% of the national vote share. This seat (Brighton Pavilion) remained the only Green seat until 2024, which is the first time they have won more than one MP. Before 2024, the Greens’ highest vote share was 3.8% in 2015, decreasing to 1.6% in 2017, before increasing to 2.7% in 2019.

#### Where did Reform and the Greens win seats?

Reform won two seats in the East Midlands (Ashfield, and Boston and Skegness) and three seats in the East of England (Clacton, Great Yarmouth, and South Basildon and East Thurrock). All Reform gains were from Conservative seats. Lee Anderson won Ashfield for Reform. He was elected for the Conservatives in Ashfield in 2019, but defected to Reform UK in March 2024.

The Greens held Brighton Pavilion (South East), gained Bristol Central (South West) from Labour and gained North Herefordshire (West Midlands) and Waveney Valley (East of England) from the Conservatives.

The majorities and vote shares for these seats are shown in the table below.

### 4.13 Seats changing hands

#### Seat gains, losses and net change, by party

303 seats out of 650 changed hands at the 2024 general election. Because of boundary changes, gains and losses are calculated relative to “notional results”, which estimate the result of the 2019 election had it been contested on the new 2024 boundaries.

The table below shows gains, losses and net change by party.

#### Seats: gains and losses, by party, 2019 to 2024

```text
                                                    Net
Party                 2019     Gains    Losses               2024
                                                 change
Labour                 200      218         7      +211      411
Conservatives          372         1      252       -251     121
Liberal Democrats        8        64        0       +64       72
SNP                     48         1       40        -39       9
Sinn Féin                7         0        0         0        7
Independent              0         6        0        +6        6
DUP                      8         0        3         -3       5
Reform UK                0         5        0        +5        5
Green                    1         3        0        +3        4
Plaid Cymru              2         2        0        +2        4
SDLP                     2         0        0         0        2
APNI                     1         1        1         0        1
Speaker                  1         0        0         0        1
TUV                      0         1        0        +1        1
UUP                      0         1        0        +1        1
```

Across the UK, Labour made the largest number of gains, winning 218 new seats while losing seven. This gave them a net gain of 211 seats. The Liberal Democrats gained 64 seats and lost none, so made a net gain of 64. The Conservatives lost 252 seats and won one new seat, giving them a net loss of 251 seats.

The Scottish National Party lost 40 of their former seats and gained only one, leading to a net loss of 39 seats.

Most Labour gains (182) were from the Conservatives. The Conservatives took only one seat from Labour: the current constituency of Leicester East.

Most Liberal Democrat gains (60) were from the Conservatives.

<!-- p. 74 -->
In Scotland, a total of 41 seats changed hands. Labour gained 36 seats (all from the Scottish National Party) and the Liberal Democrats gained four. The SNP gained one seat from the Conservatives.

In Northern Ireland, four seats changed hands. The DUP lost three seats: one each to the Alliance Party of Northern Ireland, the Ulster Unionist Party, and Traditional Unionist Voice. Alliance lost one seat (North Down) to the independent candidate, Alex Easton.

In Wales, Labour gained nine Conservative seats and the Liberal Democrats gained one. Plaid Cymru gained two seats, giving them a total of four MPs.

### 4.14 Comparing with previous elections

**Comparing Labour’s performance with 1997**

Labour won a similar number of seats to its 1997 landslide – 411 in 2024, 418 in 1997 – although, as noted above, its vote share was lower. The geographical distribution of Labour seats was also different in 2024 to 1997.

<!-- p. 80 -->

**Comparing the Liberal Democrats’ performance in 2024 and 2005**

The Liberal Democrats won 72 seats in 2024, their highest ever number. Their previous high was in 2005, when they won 62 seats.

The party won no seats in the East Midlands or the North East in 2024, where it had won seats in 2005. It also won a lower percentage of available seats in Wales, Scotland, the North West, the West Midlands, and Yorkshire and the Humber than it had in 2005.

However, the party increased its share of seats substantially in the South East of England compared with 2005, and also increased in the South West and East of England.

### 4.15 Minor parties and independents

More independent and minor party candidates stood in the 2024 general election than in 2019. Six independent candidates won election to Parliament.

#### Independent candidates

A candidate standing for election without representing a registered political party is known as an independent.

Just over twice as many independent candidates stood in the 2024 general election than in 2019. They were also more successful, with six independent MPs elected, whereas none were elected in 2019.

In the 2024 general election, there were 459 independent candidates (accounting for 10.2% of the total number of candidates). This is more than twice as many as stood in the 2019 election, where there were 224 independent candidates (6.7% of the total).

Overall, independent candidates received 564,095 votes (2.0% of total votes cast). This is an increase from 2019 when independents received 206,486 votes, accounting for 0.6% of total votes cast.

#### Successful independent candidates

Six independent candidates were elected at the 2024 general election. Five won seats in English constituencies where Labour Party candidates came in second place, and one gained a seat in Northern Ireland against an incumbent from the Alliance Party.

Of the six independent MPs, only Jeremy Corbyn had previously been an MP. He represented Islington North for the Labour Party until 2020, when he had the Labour whip suspended, and subsequently represented the seat as an independent MP.

The five successful independent candidates who won seats from the Labour Party, including Jeremy Corbyn, prominently featured their positions on the Israel-Hamas conflict in their campaigns and opposed the major parties’ positions on the conflict.

Although Alex Easton won the North Down seat as an independent, he was previously a member, and the leader, of the Democratic Unionist Party in the Northern Ireland Assembly. He stood unsuccessfully in three general elections before winning his seat in 2024. He resigned from the party in 2021 after 21 years as a member shortly after Jeffrey Donaldson was elected as leader.

Three of the six successful independent candidates in North Down, Dewsbury and Batley, and Islington North won with large majorities, receiving vote shares of over 40%, as shown in the table below.

<!-- p. 84 -->
The seats in Blackburn, Birmingham Perry Barr, and Leicester South were narrowly won with small majorities.

#### Minor parties

There were 32 minor parties in the 2024 general election that stood in 3 or more seats.

**Number of minor party candidates**

681 candidates stood for minor parties (an average of 21 candidates per party) and 444,935 votes were received (1.5% of total votes cast). This was a marked increase from 2019 when there were 19 minor parties (with an average of 9.4 candidates per party) who received 91,110 votes (0.3% of total votes cast). George Galloway, leader of the Workers Party of Britain, had won a seat in the 2019 Parliament in the Rochdale by-election in February 2024. However, this seat was regained by Labour in the 2024 general election, with George Galloway taking second place.

Birmingham Yardley was the constituency with the highest percentage of votes for minor parties, with 29.3% going to the Workers Party of Britain.

The Traditional Unionist voice fielded 14 candidates and received an average of 3,478 votes per candidate. The People Before Profit Alliance fielded three candidates and received an average of 2,813 votes per candidate.

The Scottish Libertarian Party received the fewest votes per candidate, fielding four candidates with each receiving 134 votes on average.

Candidates in a UK parliamentary election must pay a deposit: currently, this is £500. If they receive at least 5% of votes cast in their constituency then their deposit is returned. Of the 681 minor party candidates who stood in the 2024 election, 52 reached the 5% threshold.

#### Independent and minor party candidates who came a close second

In the 2024 general election five incumbent Labour MPs had previously large majorities substantially reduced by independent and minor party candidates who finished in second place.

The biggest reduction in majority where an independent or minor party candidate came second was in Bethnal Green and Stepney. Rushanara Ali previously won her seat in the former constituency of Bethnal Green and Bow with a 72.9% vote share in 2019, and she won Bethnal Green and Stepney at the 2024 election with a vote share of 34.1%. The independent candidate, Ajmal Masroor, came second with 30.5%.

Wes Streeting received just over 500 more votes than independent Leanne Mohamed to win Ilford North.

Jess Phillips’s previous majority of 10,700 votes in the former Birmingham Yardley constituency was reduced to 693 in the new constituency with the same name. She narrowly defeated the Workers Party of Britain candidate, <!-- p. 87 --> Jody McIntyre. Independent candidates also finished in second place in the Bradford West and Birmingham Ladywood seats won by Naz Shah and Shabana Mahmood.

The reductions in majorities for these MPs between 2019 and in 2024 are shown in the chart below.

### 4.16 Lost deposits

A person standing as a candidate in a UK parliamentary election must pay a deposit: this is currently £500. If they receive more than 5% of the vote in the constituency they stand in, their deposit is returned.

The deposit was last increased in 1985. The increase was from £150 to £500. The share of the vote required for candidates to have their deposits returned was also lowered from 12.5% to 5%.

#### Lost deposits by party

In the 2024 general election, there were 1,673 candidates who lost their £500 deposit (37% of all candidates). In total, £836,500 worth of deposits were forfeited to the Treasury’s Consolidated Fund.

This was a similar percentage of candidates to 2019, when 1,274 candidates (around 38% of all candidates) lost their deposits totalling £637,000. The increase was largely driven by a growth in the number of candidates not from the main parties, in particular independents. In 2024, there were 459 Independent candidates, 406 lost their deposit. In 2019 there were 224 Independents, 211 lost their deposit.

The table below shows the number of candidate deposits which were retained and forfeited at the 2024 general election.

#### Candidate deposits by party in 2024

```text
            Retained               Lost                   Value
Party        Number         Number             %               £
Lab               630              1          0.2%           500
Con               604             31          4.9%         15,500
LD                401            229         36.3%        114,500
SNP                57            -            0.0%            -
PC                 29              3          9.4%          1,500
RUK               577             32          5.3%         16,000
Green             371            258         41.0%        129,000

SF                 14            -            0.0%            -

Total            2,842         1,673         37.1%        836,500
```

In 2024, of the three largest parties in Parliament, Labour lost the fewest deposits (just one, in Westmorland and Lonsdale). The Conservatives lost 31 deposits; 16 of these were in Scotland and five in Northern Ireland.

In 2019, Labour lost 12 deposits, over half of which were in Scotland. This was an increase from 2017, when no Labour candidate lost their deposit.

<!-- p. 89 -->
Since 1945, both Labour and the Conservatives’ share of total lost deposits has generally been low. For Labour, the highest rate over this period was in 1983, when Labour lost 16.1% of its deposits. For the Conservatives, the highest rate of lost deposits was 6.3% in October 1974.

The Liberal Democrats lost 229 deposits in 2024 (36% of their candidates). This was an increase from 136 (22%) in 2019 but lower than 275 in 2017, which was the largest number of lost deposits the party had ever suffered (although not the largest proportion which was in 1950).

Reform UK lost 32 deposits (5% of its 609 candidates). 18 of these lost deposits were in London and 10 in Scotland. The Green parties lost 258 deposits in 2024 (41% of Green candidates).

**Historical trends**

In 1918, the first year that deposits were introduced, 161 candidates failed to obtain vote share necessary to retain their deposit. In the general elections between 1918 and 1935, the number of lost deposits ranged from 27 (in 1923) to 161 (in 1918).

Between 1945 and 1983, the proportion of candidates losing their deposits generally increased. In 1945, 10.8% of candidates lost their deposits. In 1983, the proportion had increased to 28.7%.

The 1987 general election was the first after the introduction of a larger deposit and the reduction in the share of the vote needed to retain the deposit. Compared with 1983, the proportion of candidates losing their deposit decreased by 16.3 percentage points.

In 2024, 37.1% of candidates forfeited their deposit. This was the lowest rate since 2001.

### 4.17 Declaration times

#### 2024 election night

When the polls close at 22:00, the BBC/ITV/Sky exit poll is released. The 2024 exit poll projected that the Labour Party would gain an overall majority of 170 seats.

The chart below shows the number of seats declared for each party as election night unfolded. Houghton and Sunderland South won the race to be the first constituency to declare at 23:18, ahead of Blyth and Ashington at 23:34 (both seats were held by Labour).

The first Liberal Democrat seat was declared in the early morning of 5 July 2024, at 01:36 when they gained Harrogate and Knaresborough from the Conservatives.

The first Conservative seat was declared at 01:47 on 5 July 2024 when they held the seat of Rayleigh and Wickford.

<!-- p. 91 -->
The final constituency to declare was Inverness, Skye and West Ross-shire. This seat was declared at 18:00 on Saturday 6 July 2024 and was a Liberal Democrat gain from the SNP. The late declaration was the result of a recount due to a discrepancy between the verified votes total and the provisional number of counted votes.

#### Historical declaration times

Thirty-seven per cent of constituencies declared results before 4:00am in the 2024 general election.

The chart below shows the percentage of constituencies declaring before 4:00am at each general election since 1983.

At general elections from 1983 to 2005, the percentage of constituencies that had declared before 4:00am never fell below 75%. ### 4.18 The weather on election day

The 2024 general election was held two weeks after the summer solstice (the longest day of the year) which meant more daylight hours and potentially better weather for voting. However, spring 2024 had been particularly wet across the UK, as was the day when the election was called .

In contrast, the 2019 election was ten days before the shortest day of the year when some parts of the UK had experienced sub-zero temperatures and snow .

Turnout in the 2024 general election was 59.6%, down from 67.3% in 2019, despite the better weather and much longer daylight hours in 2024. It was the second lowest turnout at a general election in the last 100 years. Only 2001 had a lower level at 59.4%.

**Daylight hours**

A much more predictable event than the weather is the length of daylight

```text
hours.  With polls open for 15 hours from 7am to 10pm, all parts of the UK
```

experienced sunrise well before the polls opened on the day of the general election. Longer daylight hours at an election so close to the summer solstice could potentially boost turnout.

In London, the sun set at 21:19 on polling day, and there was more than double the total length of daylight than during the 2019 general election. The sun set at 21:31 in Cardiff and 21:59 in Edinburgh. In Belfast, it set as the polls closed at 22:00. The sun set even later in Glasgow, Aberdeen and other parts of the UK further to the north and west.

### 4.19 Changes in party representation during the 2019–24 Parliament

#### State of the parties in Parliament in 2019 and 2024

After the 2019 general election, the Conservative government had a simple majority of 80 seats. The impact of by-elections and changes of party affiliation meant this was reduced to 40 by the 2024 dissolution, when the Parliament finished for the general election. The table below shows how party representation in Parliament changed between the 2019 general election and the 2024 Dissolution.

#### By-elections

There were 23 by-elections in the 2019–24 Parliament. Nine seats were held by the same parties that had won them in the 2019 general election, and in 14 of them a different party won the seat. By comparison, there were five by-elections in the 2017–19 Parliament: four holds and one gain.

The 2019–24 Parliament had the highest number of by-elections since the 1987–92 Parliament, which also had 23 by-elections. The table below shows


the constituencies, dates and levels of support for the five main parties in all 23 by-elections in the 2019–24 Parliament.

The average turnout in by-elections during the 2019–24 Parliament was 37.1%. This was lower than 2017–19 Parliament, which had an average turnout of 46.4%. The lowest turnout since 2019 was in Southend West (22.4%) and the highest was in Chesham & Amersham (52.1%) and Tiverton & Honiton (52.2%).

**Why were by-elections triggered?**

Five of the 23 by-elections were caused by the death of a sitting MP (in Chesham and Amersham, Old Bexley and Sidcup, Southend West, Birmingham Erdington, and Rochdale). The remaining 18 were due to resignations.

Reasons for these resignations can be grouped into four broad categories:

Eight resignations were for reasons relating to scandal, misconduct or criminal convictions: Hartlepool, North Shropshire, Tiverton and Honiton, Wakefield, City of Chester, Uxbridge and South Ruislip, Somerton and Frome, and Tamworth.

Three by-elections occurred after MPs who had already announced that they would not be returning after the 2024 general election resigned

```text
[chart/graphic text beside the paragraphs above]
•

•
```

<!-- p. 97 -->
earlier for personal reasons: Selby and Ainsty, Mid Bedfordshire, and Kingswood.

Four by-elections took place after MPs resigned to take up positions outside Parliament: Batley and Spen, West Lancashire, Stretford and Urmston, and Airdrie and Shotts.

Three by-elections were due to resignations of MPs following successful recall petitions: Rutherglen and Hamilton West, Wellingborough, and Blackpool South.

**Winning parties compared with the 2019 general election**

Labour won 13 by-elections during the 2019–24 Parliament, with the Conservatives and Liberal Democrats each winning four, and the Scottish National Party (SNP) and the Workers Party of Britain (WPB) each winning one.

Labour held five seats (Batley and Spen, Birmingham Erdington, City of Chester, Stretford and Urmston, and West Lancashire) and gained seven seats from the Conservatives (Wakefield, Selby and Ainsty, Mid Bedfordshire, Tamworth, Kingswood, Wellingborough and Blackpool South) and one from the SNP (Rutherglen and Hamilton West).

The Conservatives retained three seats (Old Bexley and Sidcup, Southend West, and Uxbridge and South Ruislip) and gained one from Labour (Hartlepool).

The Liberal Democrats gained all four of their seats from the Conservatives (Chesham and Amersham, North Shropshire, Tiverton and Honiton, and Somerton and Frome).

The SNP held onto their seat in Airdrie and Shotts, and the Workers Party of Britain gained a seat from Labour in Rochdale.

The chart below shows the percentage point change in the vote shares of winners of by-elections compared with the 2019 general election. Negative values represent a lower vote share in the by-election compared with the 2019 general election.

In most cases, the winning parties had substantially higher vote shares in the by-elections than in the 2019 general election. Notable examples include Rochdale, Tiverton and Honiton, and North Shropshire.

In three by-elections, the winning party won with a smaller share of the vote than they had achieved in the 2019 general election (Batley and Spen, Old Bexley and Sidcup, and Uxbridge and South Ruislip).

**By-election winners in the 2024 general election**

All those who won by-elections in the 2019–24 Parliament stood in the 2024 general election; five lost and 18 held their seat. However, constituency boundaries changed for the 2024 general election and the constituency won by an MP in 2024 may have been different from the previous seat.

All 17 Liberal Democrat and Labour by-election winners won seats again in the 2024 general election, as did one Conservative. The remaining five of the 23 winners stood again in 2024 but lost their seats; three Conservative candidates, one SNP candidate and one Workers Party of Britain candidate.

#### Changes of party affiliation

In the 2019-24 Parliament, there were 44 changes to party affiliation involving 43 different individuals. This is lower than the previous 2017-19 Parliament, when there were 60 changes of party affiliation involving 52 individuals. Collectively, in the six Parliaments prior to this (1992 to 2017), 65 individual MPs had at least one change of affiliation.

<!-- p. 99 -->
A change of party affiliation occurs if an MPresigns from their party to become an independent, if they change to another party, or if they are suspended from their parliamentary party whip (even if temporarily).

23 Conservative MPs changed party affiliation. Three MPs crossed the floor to the Labour Party and the rest became independents. 13 Labour MPs changed party affiliation in the 2019–24 Parliament; all became independents. The SNP had 5 MPs change party affiliation. Two members went to the Alba party, two became independents and one crossed the floor to the Conservatives.

One MP had two changes in party affiliation in the 2019-24 Parliament and then stood in the 2024 general election. This individual was SNP MP, Neale Hanvey. He was suspended from the SNP in November 2019 and became an independent. After a 6-month suspension and having the whip withdrawn, he rejoined the SNP. He later resigned from the SNP in March 2021 and changed party affiliation to the Alba Party. The Alba party was the party he stood for in the 2024 general election, but he lost his seat.

Lee Anderson was elected as a Conservative MP but was suspended from the party in February 2024. Following this, he was briefly an independent, before joining Reform UK in March 2024. He was re-elected as a Reform UK MP in the 2024 general election.

Reasons for the party changes were as follows. Twelve Conservative MPs had their whip withdrawn, six resigned and five were suspended. Ten Labour MPs had their whip withdrawn, two were suspended and one resigned. Two SNP MPs had their whip withdrawn and the other three resigned. The Democratic Unionist party (DUP) and Plaid Cymru (PC) each had one MP change party affiliation in the 2019-24 Parliament, with both MPs becoming independent.

#### Party changes and 2024 general election

19 of the MPs who changed party affiliation in the 2019-24 Parliament stood in the 2024 general election; 24 did not. Of those who stood, 10 were re-elected and nine lost their seats.

### 4.20 Donations to party campaigns

During the 2024 general election period (22 May to 4 July), parties received a total of £15.2 million in registered donations. Just under two-thirds (64%) of this was donated to the Labour Party.

**Which donations must be registered?**

All donations to political parties exceeding £7,500 must be registered with the Electoral Commission. These donation are published on the Electoral Commission’s searchable database . Outside election periods, donations are reported on a quarterly basis. This frequency increases to weekly during election campaigns.

<!-- p. 100 -->
#### What was the distribution of donations for the 2024 general election?

During the six weeks prior to the 2024 general election period, parties received a total of £15.2 million of registered donations. This was only half of the £30.7 million received in the six weeks prior to the 2019 general election.

In 2024, the Labour Party received the largest share of donations, compared with the Conservative party and the Liberal Democrats who both received around £1.6m in donations.

Donations to the Labour Party increased by 80% compared with 2019. Liberal Democrats also received more donations in 2024 (+29%). Donations to the Conservative Party fell by 92%.

Donations to the Green parties (registered separately in England, Scotland and Northern Ireland) decreased from £245,000 in 2019 to £160,000 in 2024 (-34%). The SNP received one individual donation with a total value of just under £128,000 during the 2024 general election campaign. This was around £100,000 more than in 2019. The majority of donations to all parties were from individuals. The Labour Party was the only party to also receive donations from trade unions.

#### Who donated to political parties prior to the 2024 general election?

Donations may be in cash or in kind and can come from individuals registered to vote in the UK, corporations registered in the country, or UK-based trade unions. Donations from individuals accounted for the highest share of all donations: £9.2 million, or 61%. Companies donated £2.4 million (16%), followed by trade unions £2.2 million (15%).

#### Notes to the data

The data is based on verification of declarations from local returning officers. Vote totals include revised declaration figures for Putney constituency, after a correction was issued by the returning officer.

Comparisons of seats won with 2019 are against the notional 2019 results for new boundaries rather than the actual number of seats won in 2019.

Electorates are correct as of election day, which may differ from those published elsewhere. Calculation of turnout is based on valid votes as a percentage of the electorate.

Sir Lindsay Hoyle stood in Chorley constituency as “The Speaker seeking re-election”, and in line with convention there were no candidates from the main parties. He is assigned “Speaker” in the Party variable.
